  • Version: 3.63 | Tool: Claude Code (claude-fable-5) | Session: claude/token-human-capital-mjrbc7 | Date: 19.06.2026 | Changes: Added §14.25 (Token Capital, Human Capital, Control Room Capital — Three Forms of Durable Value in the Agentic Era) and Key Contribution #30. Triggered by Satya Nadella's Build 2026 framing of token capital and human capital as co-accumulating assets, and Risto Päärni's lived observation after seven months of intensive HIL-phase operation (Oct 2025 – Jun 2026) in the DWS IQ 6 ecosystem: the gym returned to the schedule, indicating the HIL → HIC (human-in-the-loop → human-in-control) transition. Introduces a third capital form — Control Room Capital — the durable infrastructure and operational sovereignty produced as an architectural byproduct of the HIL phase. Key findings: (1) token capital is the organisationally-owned, reusable asset accumulated through sustained AI operations (skill corpus, evaluated agents, standards documents, validated prompts); (2) human capital deepens — not narrows — during intensive HIL: pattern recognition, prompt calibration intuition, architectural judgment, critical approach; (3) the HIL phase constitutes a competitive barrier because it cannot be shortcut — calibrated thresholds, exception-pattern libraries, and operator intuition are empirical artefacts that must be formed under real conditions; (4) the HIL → HIC transition is an observable operational milestone, not a boolean event. Standards-layer implication: the three-capital model extends the KYA Standard candidate vocabulary — token capital accumulation maps to the ASB/EAB tracks (what agents do and how they choose, both of which improve as skill corpus grows); Control Room Capital maps to the SIB track (substrate-integrity and operational sovereignty); human capital sits above the conformance vocabulary as the sovereign that sets the KYA policy parameters. Key Contribution #30 added.     • Abstract

    This thesis documents the implementation of the Firehorse Operations Model, an AI-assisted approach to e-commerce store management using Claude Code. The model demonstrates significant efficiency gains in product catalog management, Google Merchant Center integration, and payment processing through Revolut Business. We present the technical architecture, operational workflows, and future evolution toward audio-based customer interactions with industry-specific carbon calculator integration and EU regulatory compliance.

    Keywords: AI Operations, E-Commerce Automation, Google Merchant Center, Claude Code, Firehorse Model, EU AI Act Compliance, Carbon Calculator, Industrial Compliance

    • 1. Introduction

    1.1 Background

    The enterprise software market increasingly demands rapid product iteration, compliance with EU regulations, and seamless customer experiences. Traditional e-commerce operations require manual intervention for product updates, feed management, and customer support. The Firehorse Operations Model addresses these challenges through AI-native automation using Claude Code.

    Lifetime Oy operates DWS10.com, an enterprise e-commerce platform serving climate intelligence solutions, compliance software, and professional consulting services to 16 EU-regulated industries. The platform integrates with:

  • Google Merchant Center (Account ID: 8496329) for product visibility
  • Revolut Business for checkout and payment processing
  • Claude Code for automated store maintenance via Firehorse operations model
  • Cloudflare Pages for static hosting with EU data residency

    • 1.2 Research Questions

    The PhD thesis is organized around seven doctoral research questions that explicitly differentiate it from the master's thesis (Metropolia V3Thesis) in scope and ambition. The master's thesis addresses the Firehorse product manufacturing operating model; this doctoral research addresses the foundational standards, market structures, and transaction primitives that govern agentic AI at societal scale.

    Question 1 (sovereignty) is the connecting research thread, the umbrella inside which the remaining six questions acquire a shared regulatory and technological surface. Sovereignty is not a separate research area but the binding constraint that places the ontology layer, the KYA standard, the Agentic AI Foundation / NIST / EU alignment, agentic commerce, the EU eWallet, and transaction primitives into the same jurisdictional space.
  • Sovereignty — the connecting research thread (note: see _mahdolliset patentit_ framing on items 5–7) — How does a three-pillar sovereignty model (Data, Technology, Operational sovereignty) combined with a three-jurisdiction emphasis profile (EU, US, Singapore) provide the integrative frame within which the remaining six research questions become coherent? In what sense is sovereignty not a separate question but the binding constraint that gives the ontology layer, the KYA standard, the Agentic AI Foundation / NIST / EU standardization track, agentic commerce, the EU eWallet, and transaction primitives their shared regulatory and technical surface? How does the post-quantum cryptographic transition (HNDL, NIST FIPS 203/204/205, CNSA 2.0, ENISA / IMDA roadmaps) re-price each of the six remaining research questions over the 3–7 year horizon? — See §14.23 Three-Pillar Sovereignty Taxonomy.
  • Ontologies and multi-industry combinatorial benefits — How do shared ontological frameworks enable cross-industry compliance intelligence that single-vertical tools cannot produce? What industrial benefits emerge when the carbon balance sheet thesis (§1.4) formalizes emissions flows across ETS, CBAM, CSRD, and EU Taxonomy as a reusable ontology layer — and at what scale does the cross-industry network effect become self-reinforcing?
  • Know Your Agent (KYA) standard development — How should a general-purpose identity, trust, and governance standard for agentic AI systems be designed to satisfy EU AI Act Articles 9 (risk management), 12 (logging), and 14 (human oversight) while remaining adoptable by open-standards bodies (Agentic AI Foundation, Linux Foundation)? _(KYA's four-track ASB/EAB/CSB/SIB suite is the operational instrument for testing the sovereignty pillars — see §14.23.4.)_
  • Agentic AI Foundation, NIST, and EU alignment — What standardization pathway (open specification + reference implementation + multi-body adoption) enables KYA to become a foundational interoperability standard across EU regulatory, US NIST, and global agentic contexts? How must the standard be structured so that EU regulatory obligations and US NIST voluntary frameworks can both reference it without conflict? _(Jurisdiction triangulation is the same EU / US / Singapore frame as §14.23.)_
    • Mahdolliset patentit — items 5–7. The next three research questions — Agentic Commerce, EU eWallet, and Transactions — are framed jointly as a potential patent track (_mahdolliset patentit_). Each is positioned for evaluation by Lifetime Oy IP counsel during the PhD research window; the supervisor letter (§1.6) flags them explicitly for early IPR scoping.
  • Agentic Commerce — How do autonomous AI agents transact commercially on behalf of humans and enterprises within regulated EU markets? What accountability, identity-binding, and audit-trail mechanisms are required for agentic transactions to carry legal and commercial weight — and how does KYA serve as the trust primitive enabling this? _(Patent-track candidate; see §1.2 framing note.)_
  • EU eWallet — How does the EU Digital Identity Wallet (eIDAS 2.0) integrate with agentic systems to enable agent-to-agent and agent-to-institution transactions with legally valid identity and consent chains? What changes to KYA are required for it to function as the agent-side counterpart to the citizen-side eWallet? _(Patent-track candidate; see §1.2 framing note.)_
  • Transactions — What is the minimal viable transaction primitive that allows AI agents to conduct, verify, and dispute commerce across EU-regulated sectors? How does the journal-entry model (§1.4 carbon balance sheet) extend to cover non-carbon commercial transactions within an agentic commerce architecture? _(Patent-track candidate; see §1.2 framing note. Transaction primitives must additionally be quantum-safe within the §14.23.5 transition horizon.)_

    • 1.2.1 Discussion Questions for Supervisor

    The following five questions are reformulated from the thesis's early operational observations into doctoral-level propositions. They are intended as open agenda items for supervisor discussion — not yet formalised as research questions, but candidates for refinement into the research programme. The order has been reset to place the sovereignty-themed question first, in alignment with the §1.2 umbrella structure.

  • Carbon compliance signal as a recommendation primitive — scope and sovereignty — Can a cross-regulation carbon balance sheet (ETS/CBAM/CSRD/EU Taxonomy) replace intent-signal marketing with compliance-gap routing for industrial buyers at EU cross-border scale? What EU data sovereignty constraints (GDPR, EU Data Act 2023/2854, AI Act) govern the use of third-party emissions data as a commercial recommendation signal? _(Composes with the §1.2 sovereignty umbrella research question and with the §14.23 three-pillar model.)_
  • Autonomous catalog governance boundary — At what decision-making boundary should AI catalog management systems defer to human judgment in regulated EU e-commerce environments? What governance mechanism (EU AI Act Article 14; operator accountability policies) determines that boundary — and is it stable across industries, or does it need to be dynamically determined per sector and risk classification?
  • Efficiency measurement in agentic orchestration — diminishing returns and reversal — Under what conditions do efficiency gains from AI-assisted multi-channel product feed orchestration (FTE reduction, time-to-publish, error rate) saturate or reverse? What factors — hallucination rate, regulatory versioning frequency, integration drift — are the primary predictors of reversal, and can they be modelled a priori?
  • Ontological grounding as a prerequisite for auditable multimodal recommendations — What level of domain-ontology grounding is necessary for multimodal AI recommendations (voice + text) to meet EU AI Act Article 13 transparency requirements in B2B industrial procurement? Is ontological grounding sufficient, or does regulatory auditability require additional provenance mechanisms (e.g., chain-of-thought logging, source attribution)?
  • Risk classification conflicts between EU AI Act and sector-specific regulations — When EU AI Act risk classification (Articles 9, 13, 17) is applied to AI-assisted recommendation systems serving simultaneously CSRD, ETS, and CBAM-regulated industries, does the interaction produce complementary compliance obligations or irreconcilable conflicts? Which conflicts, if any, require new regulatory guidance to resolve?

    • 1.2.2 Unified DWS OS Spine and Scenario Prioritization (v3.77)

    In response to two industrial signals 48 hours apart — Dell and Palantir's "On-Premises AI Operating System" announcement of 18.05.2026, and Google's "Antigravity Agent Operating System" public demonstration of 19.05.2026 (93 subagents, 12 hours, 15,000+ model requests, 2.6 billion tokens, under USD 1,000 in API credits) — the doctoral programme is reframed around a single unifying artefactual object:

    DWS OS — the NVIDIA-based, EU-sovereign on-premises AI operating system. Already operating in 24/7 production on NVIDIA GB10 Grace Blackwell (Acer Veriton GN100), running Nemotron 3 Super (123.6 B / 12.7 B-active MoE) and Qwen3-Coder 30B via Ollama, under the OpenClaw agent runtime, with KYA (Know-Your-Agent) governance and Firecracker MicroVM hardware isolation (< 125 ms kill, AWS benchmark). Operator: Lifetime Oy, Finland.

    The six prior research questions (§1.2) are preserved as workloads on, and instantiations of, DWS OS rather than as parallel research streams. KYA (§1.2 RQ2) is the governance standard that runs inside DWS OS. The carbon balance sheet (§1.4) is the flagship workload. Agentic Commerce, EU eWallet, and Transaction primitives (§1.2 RQ4–RQ6) collapse into one monograph forward-looking chapter rather than three separate articles, on the basis that eIDAS 2.0 wallets are not operational at industrial scale within the thesis horizon.

    To make the prioritization explicit, every scenario currently on the table is scored on five axes — Originality (defensible novelty), Feasibility (artefact exists now), Publishability (clear peer-reviewed venues), Funding-magnet (Horizon / Defence / Industry money pulls toward it), and Article-1-2026-ready (can it ship a paper this year?). Scale 1–5; brutal, not flattering.

    1.4 Motivating Thesis: The Carbon Balance Sheet

    The central hypothesis motivating this doctoral research is that EU climate compliance — spanning ETS, CBAM, CSRD, and the EU Taxonomy — is not a reporting problem but a ledger problem. The same physical quantity of CO2e flows through multiple regulatory touchpoints: a steel mill's ETS surrender becomes an importer's CBAM embedded emission, becomes a construction company's CSRD Scope 3 upstream disclosure, and determines their Taxonomy alignment. Current compliance tools treat each regulation as a separate reporting workflow, producing duplicated data, inconsistent factors, and ±40% Scope 3 uncertainty.

    This thesis proposes that carbon compliance requires double-entry bookkeeping — the same architectural solution that financial accounting adopted centuries ago. Every compliance event produces a journal entry (debit or credit) in dual units (EUR and tCO2e). The resulting carbon balance sheet provides a net carbon position that is auditable, reconcilable, and machine-readable.

    Why this frames the Firehorse operations research:

    The Firehorse Operations Model (Sections 3–13) documents the engineering infrastructure — multi-agent orchestration, edge deployment, ERP integration, regulatory intelligence — required to build a carbon balance sheet that works across 20 EU-regulated industries. Without the harness architecture (Section 14.12), the cross-industry ledger cannot maintain audit integrity. Without the MCP orchestration, the cross-regulation reconciliation cannot happen in real-time. Without the edge deployment, industrial sites cannot contribute verified emissions data.

    Three research questions emerge from the carbon balance sheet thesis:
  • Structural isomorphism: Is the analogy between financial double-entry bookkeeping and carbon compliance tracking formally valid, or merely metaphorical? Can the same data structures, reconciliation algorithms, and audit guarantees transfer?
  • Cross-vertical intelligence: Does a multi-industry carbon ledger produce emergent intelligence that single-vertical tools cannot replicate? Specifically: when a steel mill's verified ETS emissions replace a construction company's CBAM default values, does the accuracy improvement (hypothesized ±3% vs ±40%) materialize in practice?
  • Network effects in compliance data: At what scale (number of industries, entities, transactions) does the cross-regulation reconciliation become self-reinforcing — where adding one more industry to the ledger improves data quality for all existing industries?
    • The role of open source connectors:

    A carbon balance sheet requires verified data from heterogeneous industrial systems — ERP platforms (SAP, IFS, Dynamics, Visma, Jeeves), EU registries (EUTL, CBAM Transitional Registry), emission factor databases (EEA, IPCC), IoT sensors, and ESG platforms (Worldfavor, PositionGreen). This thesis argues that these connectors must be open source (MIT-licensed) for three reasons: (1) auditability — auditors must inspect the data path from source system to ledger entry, not trust a vendor's black box; (2) network effect acceleration — open source lowers adoption barriers, enabling the cross-vertical intelligence to emerge faster; (3) regulatory defensibility — EU AI Act Article 12 requires auditable AI pipelines, which open source satisfies by design. The connector architecture extends the ERP integration patterns already implemented in DWS (`src/integrations/erp/`), treating each connector as a standardized adapter (input schema → journal entry) that plugs into the ledger's append-only ingestion API.

    These questions extend beyond the Firehorse e-commerce operations into the domain of compliance infrastructure design — the transition from "tools that generate reports" to "infrastructure that maintains a system of record for carbon." The DWS 7 implementation (Phase 2.6, PRs 093A–093H) provides the empirical test bed.

    1.5 Research Focus Refinement (v2.10)

    The refined research focus is organized into three streams:

  • KYA as an Agentic AI Foundation standard: KYA (Know Your Agent) is elevated from an internal governance wrapper toward a generalizable foundation standard for identity binding, runtime control, auditability, and sovereignty guarantees in agentic systems.
  • DWS 7 objectives as empirical testbed: DWS 7 is treated as the operational environment to validate carbon-ledger infrastructure, KYA governance, multi-partner integration (Siemens, NVIDIA Connect), and sovereignty-aware product segmentation.
  • Dual-use industries as the third research stream: The third stream is explicitly focused on dual-use industries (industrial + defence), including ongoing Nokia Defence discussions as part of the funding and pilot pathway.

    • A continuity note is added from DWS 5 to DWS 7: hydrogen economy compliance (RFNBO additionality and verification) is treated as a longitudinal thread in the platform's research program.

    Product-tier and sovereignty segmentation (core figure):

    The figure anchors three product layers in the research framing:

  • Aegis = security and critical-infrastructure governance product
  • Sovereign Infra = EU-sovereign infrastructure layer for regulated operators
  • Connect = global integration and distribution layer
    • Open-source standardization path (new refinement):
  • KYA standard proposal to Agentic AI Foundation / Linux Foundation is currently in draft state (not yet submitted).
  • NIST track is also not yet submitted and is modeled as a parallel validation path (US validation vs open-source adoption).
  • Research principle: a standard that remains private cannot scale to ecosystem adoption; therefore, standardization work must include both open specification and reference implementation evidence.

    1.7 Ontology — Etymology and Philosophical Background

    The doctoral programme situates the Firehorse operations model inside a formal enterprise ontology — the DWS Ontology Standard (see `ONTOLOGY.md` v1.3 in the project repository). To anchor the engineering and regulatory layers in their conceptual heritage, this subsection reviews the origin of the term.

    Etymology. The word _ontology_ derives from two Greek roots: ὄν (ón) — "that which is", the present participle of _einai_ ("to be") — and λόγος (lógos) — "doctrine, account, rational explanation". Literally, ontology is "the doctrine of being". Philosophical background. Ontology is one of the central branches of metaphysics. In classical Greece, philosophers such as Aristotle examined what it means for something to _be_, what kinds of entities exist (objects, properties, relations, events), and what the basic structure of being is. The term _ontology_ itself was consolidated only in the 17th–18th centuries, within scholastic and early-modern philosophy, when metaphysics was systematised into distinct sub-fields. Modern usage. Today the term is applied across philosophy (the study of the fundamental nature of existence), computer science and artificial intelligence (a formal model of concepts and the relations between them — classes, properties, relations), and the philosophy of science (the question of which entities a given theory commits to). The original meaning — _structuring what is_ — is preserved while the application surface shifts. The DWS Ontology Standard builds on this foundation: EU regulations (ETS, CBAM, CSRD, AI Act, NIS2, GDPR) and industrial concepts are structured into a machine-readable form that humans and agents query through the same interface, enabling the carbon balance sheet and the KYA-certified governance model that the doctoral research validates.

    2. Literature Review

    2.1 AI in E-Commerce Operations

    Recent advances in Large Language Models (LLMs) enable natural language interfaces for complex technical operations. Claude Code, built on Anthropic's Claude model, provides:

  • File system manipulation
  • Code generation and modification
  • External API integration
  • Multi-step task orchestration
  • Git version control integration

    • 2.2 Product Information Management

    Modern Product Information Management (PIM) systems require:

  • Centralized data storage (products.json)
  • Multi-channel syndication (Google Merchant, social commerce)
  • Compliance metadata (EU regulations)
  • Dynamic pricing capabilities
  • Checkout link integration

    3. Firehorse Operations Model

    The Firehorse Operations Model is a human-AI collaborative framework for e-commerce management:

    ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐

│                                                              │

    3.2 Core Principles

  • Natural Language Interface: Operations expressed in plain language
  • Version Control: All changes tracked via Git
  • Audit Trail: Complete history for EU AI Act compliance
  • Reproducibility: Documented processes for consistency
  • Traceability: Decision lineage for regulatory requirements

    4.3 Feed Generation Pipeline

    // generate-feed.cjs workflow
const generateFeed = () => {
  // 1. Load products.json
  const products = require("../config/products.json");

    // 2. Validate required fields   validateProducts(products); // id, mpn, price, brand
    

    // 3. Generate XML with g: namespace   let xml = generateXMLHeader();
    

    for (const product of products) {     xml += generateProductItem(product);
    

    // 4. Add checkout_link_template for Revolut     const checkoutLink = product.revolutLink || "https://revolut.me/lifetimeoy";     xml += `<g:checkout_link_template>${checkoutLink}</g:checkout_link_template>`;   }
    

    // 5. Write google-products.xml   writeFile("google-products.xml", xml); };

  • Hosting: Cloudflare Pages (static HTML)
  • Build: Automatic deployment on git push
  • Caching: Edge caching for performance
  • Compliance: EU data residency (Finland/Germany)

    AttributeRequirementImplementation
    g:idUnique identifierMatches products.json ID
    g:mpnManufacturer part numberFormat: CATEGORY-TYPE-YEAR
    g:brandProduct brand"Lifetime" or "Firehorse"
    g:pricePrice with currencyEUR format
    g:availabilityStock statusAlways "in_stock" for services
    g:checkout_link_templatePayment URLRevolut checkout link
    g:conditionProduct conditionAlways "new"

    6.3 SFTP Upload Process

    # Weekly feed upload to Google Merchant Center
curl -T google-products.xml \
  -u "mc-sftp-8496329:PASSWORD" \
  --insecure \
  "sftp://partnerupload.google.com:19321/google-products.xml"
    Password encoding: Special characters must be URL-encoded
  • `$` becomes `%24`
  • `!` becomes `%21`

    • 6.4 Historical Issues and Resolutions

    IssueRoot CauseResolution
    Domain mismatchURLs pointing to lifetime.fiUpdated all URLs to dws10.com
    Duplicate productsID mismatch with Merchant CenterAligned IDs in products.json
    identifier_exists errorSet false when MPN+brand existRemoved field entirely
    Image URL errorsDouble slashes in pathsFixed path generation
    Missing checkout linksNo payment URL configuredAdded Revolut links

    7. EU Regulatory Compliance

    7.1 EU AI Act Requirements (August 2026)

    The EU AI Act classifies AI systems by risk level. Our audio chat and recommendation system falls under Limited Risk category, requiring:

  • Transparency: Inform users they're interacting with AI
  • Human oversight: Escalation path to human support
  • Documentation: System capabilities and limitations
  • Data protection: GDPR-compliant data handling
  • Reasoning lineage: Traceable decision chains

    7.3 Article 12 Compliance (Automatic Logging)

    The Firehorse Operations Model ensures EU AI Act Article 12 compliance through:

  • Git version control: All changes logged with timestamps
  • Commit history: Decision provenance documented
  • Feed generation logs: Processing records maintained
  • Audit trail: Complete history for regulatory review

- Fit for 55 (carbon reporting)
- CSRD (sustainability disclosure)
- CBAM (import carbon pricing)
- NIS2 (cybersecurity)

    Generated Bundle: 1. DWS IQ Platform v6 (€4,999) - Core compliance 2. Firehorse Report (€1,999) - Automated reporting 3. Consulting Fit55 (€3,000/mo) - Expert guidance 4. Protonmail Implementation (€499) - Secure communications
    

    Total: €10,497 + €3,000/mo ongoing Savings vs. individual: 15%

    12. IPR Protection & Trade Secret Strategy

    12.1 Three-Tier IPR Framework

    The Firehorse Operations Model and its underlying technologies require a structured intellectual property protection strategy. Lifetime Oy employs a three-tier classification framework:

    TierMechanismScopeLegal Basis
    Tier 1: PublicFree distributionTeaser, FCD concept, market sizing—
    Tier 2: NDA RequiredConfidentiality agreementLWM architecture, knowledge graph overview, BYOC logicEU Trade Secrets Directive 2016/943
    Tier 3A: PatentPatent protectionCIDM method, causal reasoning algorithmPRH (Finland), EPO, PCT
    Tier 3B: Trade SecretInternal controlsKnowledge graph structure (10,000+ nodes), weights, training dataEU 2016/943, Finnish Trade Secrets Act 595/2018

    12.2 NDA Gate for Technical Disclosure

    Before any technical deep-dive with partners, investors, or customers, the receiving party must sign the Lifetime Oy NDA (EU Trade Secrets Directive 2016/943 compliant). The NDA covers:

  • LWM Architecture — component diagram, data flow, processing pipeline
  • Knowledge Graph Structure — node taxonomy, edge types, relationship model
  • BYOC (Bring Your Own Cloud) Logic — cloud deployment, tenant isolation, data residency
  • Agent Orchestration — MCP server configuration, coordination protocols
  • Integration Architecture — ERP connectors (SAP, IFS, Dynamics)

    • Public/marketing materials (Tier 1) can be shared freely without NDA: product teasers, FCD concept overview, market sizing, and high-level architecture diagrams.

    12.3 Patent Strategy

    Patentable innovations are filed through PRH (Patentti- ja rekisterihallitus, Finland) with international PCT extension:

    PriorityInnovationFiling PathStatus
    P1CIDM — Climate-Intelligent Decision MakingPRH → PCT → EPO/USPTOPatent pending
    P2Causal Reasoning for Regulatory RiskPRH → PCTPrior art search Q2 2026
    P3LWM Temporal Latent SpacePRH → PCTEvaluation Q3 2026
    Critical rule: Patent applications MUST be filed before any public disclosure of the method. NDA-protected disclosures do not destroy patentability.

    12.4 Trade Secret as Preferred Protection for Knowledge Graph

    For the knowledge graph (10,000+ nodes), trade secret protection is strategically preferred over patents:

    FactorPatentTrade Secret
    Duration20 years (then public)Indefinite
    DisclosureMust publish full methodNo disclosure
    Competitor awarenessKnows exactly what's protectedCannot know what to copy
    Cost€120k–200k (3 patents, EU+US)Administrative only

    Trade secret status under EU Directive 2016/943 requires: (1) the information is secret, (2) it has commercial value because it is secret, and (3) reasonable steps have been taken to keep it secret. Lifetime Oy maintains access controls, encryption, employee confidentiality agreements, and internal classification registers.

    12.5 Implications for Firehorse Operations

    The IPR framework directly impacts Firehorse e-commerce operations:

  • Product descriptions on dws10.com describe _what_ the platform does (Tier 1), never _how_ it works
  • Investor pitch decks share problem/solution/market freely; architecture details require NDA
  • Conference presentations discuss EU compliance gaps, not algorithm specifics
  • Customer demos operate under NDA with watermarked materials
  • Store product pages reference capabilities, not implementation details

    • 13. Prompt Caching Architecture for AI Agents

    13.1 Engineering Principle: Cache Rules Everything

    The Firehorse Operations Model relies on long-running AI agent sessions (Claude Code) for store management, product updates, and compliance automation. Prompt caching is the foundational engineering principle that makes these sessions economically viable.

    Source: Thariq Shihipar (Anthropic), "Lessons from Building Claude Code: Prompt Caching Is Everything" (2026). Core insight: Prompt caching reuses computation from previous API roundtrips via prefix matching. The API caches everything from the start of the request up to each `cache_control` breakpoint. A high cache hit rate reduces costs and latency significantly.

    13.2 System Prompt Layout for Caching

    The order of prompt content matters — static content first, dynamic content last:

    1. Static system prompt & Tools     (globally cached)
2. CLAUDE.md / project config       (cached within a project)
3. Session context                   (cached within a session)
4. Conversation messages             (new per turn)
    Fragility warning: Cache ordering breaks when:
  • Timestamps are placed in the static system prompt
  • Tool definitions are shuffled non-deterministically
  • Tool parameters are updated mid-session (e.g., available agents)

    • 13.3 Six Engineering Rules for Agent Caching

    RulePrincipleImplementation
    1. Static first, dynamic lastMaximize shared prefix across sessionsSystem prompt → CLAUDE.md → session context → messages
    2. Use system messages for updatesNever modify the static prompt mid-sessionPass time, file changes, mode switches via `` tags in the next message
    3. Never change models mid-sessionPrompt caches are model-specificSwitching from Opus to Haiku at 100k tokens costs more than keeping Opus (cache rebuild)
    4. Never add/remove tools mid-sessionTools are part of the cached prefixUse tool stubs with `defer_loading: true` for optional tools; discover via ToolSearch
    5. Design features around the cacheState transitions via tools, not tool set changesPlan mode = system message + ExitPlanMode tool, not swapping the tool set
    6. Fork operations share parent prefixCompaction/summarization must reuse cacheUse identical system prompt, tools, and message history for forked requests

    13.4 Compaction: Cache-Safe Context Forking

    When running agents approach the context window limit, compaction (summarization) must preserve the cache:

    WRONG: New API call with different system prompt + no tools
       → Full cache miss, expensive

    RIGHT: Same system prompt + same tools + parent message history        + compaction prompt appended as new user message        → Cache hit on parent prefix, only new tokens are the prompt

    This requires reserving a "compaction buffer" — enough room in the context window for the compact message and summary output tokens.

    13.5 Operational Impact on Firehorse

    For DWS10.com store operations, these caching principles translate to:

    OperationCache ImpactPractice
    Product catalog updatesLong session, many file editsKeep same model throughout
    Feed generationTool-heavy workflowAll tools defined at session start
    Compliance checkingContext-heavy (regulations)CLAUDE.md cached as project config
    Multi-file sync (dual folder)Sequential dependent operationsChain in single session, don't fork
    Monitoring: Cache hit rate is treated as an uptime metric. A drop in cache hits triggers investigation — it indicates a system prompt change, tool definition shift, or model switch that needs correction.

    13.6 Cost & Latency Implications

    ScenarioCache Hit RateRelative CostLatency
    Optimized (static prefix)>90%1x (baseline)Low
    Tool set changes mid-session~50%~2xHigh
    Model switch mid-session0% (full rebuild)~3-5xVery high
    Compaction without parent prefix0%~3xHigh

    These economics are critical for Firehorse's AI-assisted store operations, where sessions routinely process 30+ products across multiple files.

    14. Results and Discussion

    14.1 Per-Industry Open LLM Selection

    No single open LLM is optimal for all 25+ EU industries served by DWS IQ. Each industry vertical has distinct regulatory language, domain knowledge requirements, and structured output needs. The `AgentConfig.model` field is already per-agent, so each concrete agent can be configured with a different trained Lifetime World Model (LWM) + open LLM, with continuous benchmarking.

    Candidate open LLMs by industry cluster:
    Industry ClusterCandidate ModelsKey Domain Requirement
    Construction, Real EstateLlama 3.3 70B, Qwen 2.5 72B, Mistral LargeEN 15978 lifecycle stages, Nordic building codes, EPBD
    Energy, Steel & Metals, CementLlama 3.3, DeepSeek-V3, Mixtral 8x22BETS allowance calculations, process emissions, CBAM
    Manufacturing, ChemicalsQwen 2.5 72B, Llama 3.3CBAM product classification, BOM analysis, REACH
    Logistics, Maritime, AviationMistral Large, Llama 3.3, Gemma 2 27BFuelEU Maritime, CORSIA, route optimization
    Agriculture, Food & BeverageLlama 3.3, Gemma 2, Command R+LULUCF, Scope 3 supply chain, packaging lifecycle
    Finance, Public SectorMistral Large (EU-hosted), Llama 3.3SFDR, EU Taxonomy, double materiality, GPP
    Healthcare, TextilesGemma 2 (EU-hosted), Qwen 2.5Digital product passport, pharmaceutical supply chain
    Selection criteria: domain knowledge depth, multilingual capability, structured output reliability, context window (128k+), inference speed, EU data residency, and commercial license. Continuous benchmarking process: Each industry agent runs weekly automated compliance prompts (EN 15978, CSRD ESRS E1, CBAM declarations) against the current model and 2-3 alternatives. If an alternative consistently scores higher on accuracy and structured output, the model is flagged for review and potential switch.

    14.2 Bring Your Own Agent (BYOA) Architecture

    Enterprise customers bring their own AI agents to work on DWS IQ. This section uses NCC (Nordic construction company) as an illustrative case.

    NCC agent ecosystem:
    Agent SourceCountExamples
    NCC Internal7Procurement, Safety Compliance, BIM, Sustainability, Cost Estimation, Quality, Project Planning
    Contractor Agents14HVAC Subcontractor, Electrical, Concrete Supplier, Steel Supplier, Window Manufacturer, Insulation, etc.
    DWS IQ Platform3Compliance Calculation, Firehorse Report, Carbon Calculator
    Total24All connected via A2A + MCP protocols
    Tenant-specific Lifetime World Model (LWM):

    The Lifetime World Model is the enterprise learning layer that sits on top of the chosen open LLM. It is not simply a fine-tuned model — it is a strategic intelligence layer that:

  • Fine-tunes enterprise data and regulation — ingests the customer's operational data, industry standards, and EU regulatory context to create domain-specific intelligence that generic LLMs cannot provide
  • Optimizes market value — continuously learns from compliance outcomes, energy efficiency results, and operational metrics to identify value creation opportunities
  • Ensures competitive advantage with multi-agent tools — combining tenant-specific knowledge with multi-agent orchestration (A2A + MCP), the LWM transforms raw AI capability into an irreplaceable operational asset

    • Each enterprise tenant receives their own LWM instance:

  • Base LLM: Selected per industry evaluation (e.g., Qwen 2.5 72B for Nordic construction)
  • Fine-tuned on: Tenant's own project data, industry codes (RT-kortisto), regulatory context
  • Connectors: Tekla Structures (BIM), IFS Cloud (ERP), CO2data.fi (emissions factors)
  • Sandbox: Stage 2 ephemeral containers (europe-north1) or Stage 3 persistent (at scale)
    • Why 20 industries make the LWM stronger: Each industry added to the platform makes every existing industry's LWM smarter through cross-pollination of anonymized compliance patterns. A carbon reduction technique proven in cement manufacturing generates transferable insight for construction. Supply chain Scope 3 agents in manufacturing transfer to food & beverage. The 20th industry customer benefits from all 19 industries' accumulated intelligence — creating a moat that single-industry competitors cannot replicate.

    14.2.1 LWM Product-Market Validation: Energy Sector Case Study (March 2026)

    The Lifetime World Model's commercial viability received its first external validation in March 2026 through partnership negotiations with Qantic GmbH (Berlin), a BESS simulation company whose Q-System models energy revenue but lacks compliance cost calculation.

    Validated hypothesis: The LWM's regulatory intelligence graph has commercial value beyond construction. Qantic's enterprise clients (e.g., MAN Energy Solutions) span six EU-regulated industries simultaneously — Power & Heat, Iron & Steel, Maritime Shipping, Road Transport, Construction, and Chemicals. DWS IQ's 20-industry LWM addresses all six from one platform, validating the cross-industry network effects thesis. Quantified output (50MW/200MWh BESS, Germany): €21,693/year total compliance cost (Scope 2: €5,005, CSRD reporting: €12,000, EU Taxonomy certification: €4,200, efficiency loss: €488). Calculated by the existing BESS Compliance API (19 unit tests passing) — not a roadmap item. Firehorse Operations Model evidence: The deal structure (€15,990 setup + €99/asset/month API) validates the Firehorse product manufacturing approach: standardized products built before customer engagement, delivered at 10x lower cost than enterprise alternatives (€200k+/year from Big 4). This demonstrates that the Firehorse model scales beyond construction into energy sector compliance — supporting the doctoral thesis that AI-native operations models are transferable across EU-regulated industries. Key Contribution #16: First external market validation of LWM commercial value outside the construction sector, confirming the 20-industry architecture creates defensible competitive advantage through cross-sector regulatory coverage.

    14.5 DWS7 Agent Architecture Modernization

    The DWS6-to-DWS7 transition addresses six critical architectural gaps discovered during production deployment. Implemented in a single Claude Code session (24.02.2026), these fixes transform the agent system from a 2023-era pattern to a production-grade agentic platform.

    The six fixes:
    #GapFixKey File
    1Single-shot LLM callsObserve-think-act loop (`runLoop()`)`base.agent.ts`
    2No tool useTool runtime with JSON Schema + 4 built-in tools`tools.ts`
    3Hardcoded Groq fetchMulti-provider client (Anthropic > Groq > OpenAI)`llm-client.ts`
    4No agent communicationDelegation + depth tracking + discovery API`index.ts`
    5MCP was vaporJSON-RPC 2.0 server at `/mcp``agent-server.ts`
    6Express-onlyEvent bus with pub/sub + Redis Streams`event-bus.ts`
    Build for Agents principle: Every DWS product must be accessible via:
  • CLI (`cli/src/cli.ts`) — JSON output, pipeable, zero dependencies
  • MCP (`/mcp` endpoint) — auto-discovered by Claude Code, Cursor
  • Claude Code Skills (`.claude/commands/`) — natural language agent invocation
  • REST API (`/api/agents/`) — traditional HTTP integration
  • Event bus — real-time agent coordination via SSE
    • EU AI Act Article 12 compliance: The agentic loop architecture inherently satisfies automatic logging requirements. Each iteration records observation, thought, actions, and results to the `agent_loop_iterations` Supabase table — creating complete reasoning lineage from input to output. Impact on Firehorse operating model: The CLI and Skills layer means the Firehorse store management workflow can now be automated by AI agents end-to-end: compliance checks via `/compliance-check`, carbon analysis via `/carbon-analysis`, and multi-agent workflows via `/workflow` — all without human GUI interaction.

    14.6 Agent Foundry Stage 1.5: OpenSandbox + EMPO² Agent Memory (v1.5)

    _Session: claude/sandbox-agents-opensandbox-HatfC | Date: 01.03.2026_

    14.6.1 Stage 1.5 — OpenSandbox on Acer Veriton GN100

    The Agent Foundry received a new isolation stage between the demo-grade SDK sandbox (Stage 1) and Cloud Run (Stage 2): OpenSandbox (https://github.com/alibaba/OpenSandbox) deployed on the Acer Veriton GN100 edge node (120 GiB RAM, 20 cores, Helsinki Lab).

    StageMethodCostUse Case
    1SDK sandbox (bubblewrap/seatbelt)€0Demo, single-tenant
    1.5OpenSandbox Docker (Veriton GN100)€0 HWEU air-gapped, internal pilot
    2Cloud Run ephemeral (europe-north1)~€20-50/moPaid multi-tenant
    3Persistent containers (gVisor)€200+/moScale

    Activation: `SANDBOX_STAGE=opensandbox`. Six MCP tools (`sandbox_create`, `sandbox_execute`, `sandbox_file_read`, `sandbox_file_write`, `sandbox_destroy`, `sandbox_ping`) expose it via `/sandbox/` endpoints in the MCP Hub.

    Firehorse operating model impact: Stage 1.5 enables EU air-gapped agent execution for regulated industries (defense, healthcare, critical infrastructure) — customers where data must never leave on-premises hardware. The Acer Veriton GN100 is the first physical Lifetime Fleet node fulfilling this capability.

    14.6.2 EMPO²: Academic Grounding for the LWM Memory Architecture

    The paper _"Exploratory Memory-Augmented LLM Agent via Hybrid On- and Off-Policy Optimization"_ (EMPO², arXiv:2602.23008, February 2026) provides academic validation for the DWS Lifetime World Model's hybrid memory design.

    EMPO² proposes combining:

  • Parametric memory (RL updates to model weights) — analogous to DWS LWM fine-tuning (QLoRA, Acer Veriton GN100)
  • Non-parametric memory (retrieval from memory buffer) — analogous to DWS RAG over EU regulations + enterprise data (pgvector)

    • Results: 128.6% improvement over GRPO on ScienceWorld, 11.3% on WebShop. Key insight: _models trained with memory become robust even without memory at inference_ — validating the DWS strategy of progressively embedding domain knowledge into LWM weights while maintaining RAG as a fallback.

    The OpenSandbox environment additionally serves as a safe EMPO²-style RL training sandbox: isolated containers provide the clean, reproducible environments needed for RL-based memory training without affecting production infrastructure.

    14.6.3 Lifetime Scope Signals for LWM Training Coordination

    _Session: claude/pedantic-easley | Date: 07.03.2026_

    The Lifetime Scope Signals (LSS) protocol — originally designed for deterministic multi-agent development coordination (see Metropolia thesis Section 2.5.2) — extends naturally to ML pipeline orchestration. Rather than introducing a dedicated ML workflow engine (Kubeflow, Airflow), the LWM training pipeline is coordinated using the same four signal types: READY, COMPLETE, ERROR, BLOCKED.

    Training Agent Fleet (T-01 through T-10):
    Agent IDNameRoleEmitsListens For
    T-01InfraVerifierValidate CUDA/PyTorch on GN-100 nodes`COMPLETE:infra-verified`(none — entry point)
    T-02ModelFetcherDownload base model to shared storage`READY:base-model``T-01:COMPLETE:infra-verified`
    T-03CurriculumAgentClaude API curriculum design per vertical`READY:curriculum:{vertical}``T-02:READY:base-model`
    T-04DataSynthAgentGenerate 2000+ synthetic examples`READY:dataset:{vertical}``T-03:READY:curriculum:{vertical}`
    T-05TrainingAgentQLoRA fine-tuning on assigned node`READY:lora-weights:{vertical}``T-04:READY:dataset:{vertical}`, `T-06:READY:node-slot`
    T-06ResourceArbiterAllocate GN-100 node slots (2 tokens)`READY:node-slot``T-05:READY:lora-weights:` (releases slot)
    T-07EvalAgentClaude-as-judge, quality gate ≥7.0`READY:eval-pass:{vertical}` / `ERROR:eval-fail:{vertical}``T-05:READY:lora-weights:{vertical}`
    T-08MergeAgentMerge LoRA adapter into base model`READY:merged-model:{vertical}``T-07:READY:eval-pass:{vertical}`
    T-09DeployAgentDeploy inference API`COMPLETE:lwm-deployed:{vertical}``T-08:READY:merged-model:{vertical}`
    T-10PipelineOrchestratorMonitor all verticals`COMPLETE:lwm-all-verticals`All `T-09:COMPLETE:`

    The `{vertical}` parameterized milestone enables per-industry pipeline instantiation — the only protocol extension over base LSS v1.0.

    Key mechanisms:
  • Quality gate signals — T-07 emits `READY:eval-pass:{vertical}` only when Claude-as-judge score ≥7.0; failure emits `ERROR:eval-fail:{vertical}`, triggering curriculum re-generation (max 3 retries → `BLOCKED` → human escalation). This maps to the EMPO² off-policy update pattern (Section 14.6.2).
  • Resource arbitration — T-06 manages two node-slot tokens (one per GN-100). Training agents acquire a token before starting; release on completion. First-come-first-served by signal emission order.
  • Fan-Out parallelism — Curriculum generation (T-03) and data synthesis (T-04) run across all 25+ verticals simultaneously (CPU-bound, no GPU constraint), eliminating idle GPU time.
    • Firehorse operating model impact: The training coordination validates LSS as a general-purpose deterministic coordination mechanism — the same protocol orchestrates both software development agents (Appendix F) and ML training pipelines, reducing operational complexity for Firehorse customers who need both capabilities.

    14.6.4 Agent Memory Fact Extraction Pipeline (PR-254)

    _Session: claude/agent-memory-fact-extraction-gLEss | Date: 23.03.2026_

    The EMPO² theoretical framework (Section 14.6.2) was operationalized through a fact extraction pipeline — the first production implementation of the non-parametric memory update path in the Firehorse operating model.

    Dual Memory Architecture:
    LayerRoleFirehorse Impact
    LWMDomain knowledge (regulations, emission factors, industry models)Enables _what_ the agent can analyze
    Client MemoryRelationship knowledge (preferences, decisions, open issues)Enables _personalized_ analysis per customer

    The fact extraction pipeline converts unstructured conversation text into structured subject-predicate-value triples using LLM-based extraction (Claude Haiku), with confidence scoring, duplicate detection via cosine similarity (pgvector), and persistence as `fact` type memories in the semantic_memories table.

    Three Execution Layers for Actionable Memory:

    Fact extraction creates passive knowledge. The Firehorse operating model requires three additional layers:

  • Firehorse State Machine — Acts on extracted facts. A fact "customer deadline Q2 2026" must trigger workflow escalation, not just sit in a database. This is the core Firehorse execution layer.
  • ERP Connector Layer — Feeds live data from Visma/IFS/SAP/Dynamics into memory. Client memory captures stated preferences; ERP reveals actual behavior (order patterns, payment history).
  • KYA Governance Wrapper (v1.4 internal / v1.1 public) — Wraps every memory mutation with audit-grade provenance. Critical insight: contradiction resolution (PR-255) without KYA attestation means resolving memory conflicts with no audit trail for _why_ one fact superseded another. The court-defensible audit chain: "fact A replaced fact B at timestamp X, approved by agent Y, logged to Firehorse" — required for CSRD and EU AI Act Article 12 compliance.
    • KYA-First PR Ordering:
    PriorityPRFeature
    1254Fact Extraction ✅
    2255Contradiction Resolution
    3—KYA Memory Audit Trail
    4257Decay Scoring
    5256Consolidation Cron
    6259Connector Sync
    7258Supermemory MCP EU
    Key Contribution #17: First production fact extraction pipeline for EU-compliant industrial AI, demonstrating dual LWM/client-memory architecture and identifying the three-layer execution gap (state machine, ERP connectors, KYA governance) essential for court-defensible enterprise AI memory in regulated industries.

    15. Conclusion

    13.1 Summary

    The Firehorse Operations Model demonstrates that AI-assisted e-commerce management can:

  • Reduce operational overhead by 90%+ through automated product management
  • Ensure compliance with EU regulations via documented audit trails
  • Enable rapid iteration on product offerings with natural language interfaces
  • Prepare infrastructure for voice-based customer interactions
  • Support industrial customers with carbon calculator integration

    • 13.2 Key Contributions

    This thesis contributes:

  • A documented framework for AI-assisted store operations (Firehorse Model)
  • Integration patterns for Google Merchant Center and Revolut Business
  • A roadmap for audio chat with carbon calculator integration (4 phases)
  • EU compliance mapping for industrial software products
  • Product recommendation engine architecture for 25+ EU industries
  • Proton products security strategy for GDPR/NIS2 compliance
  • Agent Foundry Stage 1.5 — OpenSandbox on Acer Veriton GN100 for EU air-gapped agent sandboxing
  • EMPO² alignment — hybrid parametric/non-parametric memory architecture validated by arXiv:2602.23008
  • CEO Automation Agent Suite — Captain Briefing, LinkedIn Scheduler, EU Regulation Monitor; Firehorse model applied to revenue workflow automation
  • Multi-Partner AI Ecosystem — NVIDIA, Siemens, ABB, xAI, Mistral + multi-LLM router; validates distribution hypothesis of the Firehorse operating model
  • LSS Training Pipeline Coordination — Lifetime Scope Signals applied to LWM training: Training Agent Fleet (T-01–T-10), quality gate signals, resource arbitration for 2× GN-100 nodes; validates LSS as general-purpose coordination beyond software development
  • Customer-Facing MVP Features — API Key Management, Usage Metering with 3-tier billing, Compliance Report Export; Firehorse operations model transition to customer-deliverable SaaS
  • PostgreSQL Columnar HTAP Architecture — Single-database HTAP via pg_duckdb + TimescaleDB on Aiven EU; DWS Data Standard creation governing storage engines, extension registry, retention policies, EU regulatory compliance; 4-phase rollout for Firehorse operations data
  • Two-Tier HTAP Architecture — Aiven-first mandate, TimescaleDB Hypercore for production, pg_duckdb for edge analytics; validates managed-provider constraint as architectural driver
  • Harness Engineering and Forensic Telemetry — court-defensible harness via VERSION AND AGENT HISTORY, KYA Standard as hardware-attested harness
  • LWM Product-Market Validation — Qantic GmbH energy sector case study, first external market validation
  • Agent Memory Fact Extraction Pipeline — LLM-based fact extraction, dual memory architecture, KYA-first ordering
  • Carbon Balance Sheet Thesis — EU climate compliance as ledger problem; double-entry bookkeeping for CO2 with dual-unit entries (EUR + tCO2e); three PhD research questions: structural isomorphism, cross-vertical intelligence, network effects in compliance data; DWS 7 Phase 2.6 as empirical test bed

    • DWS Platform Standards (Internal)

    The following internal standards govern the DWS IQ 6 platform architecture, security, and quality. They are maintained as living documents in the private repository and are referenced here in Oxford notation for academic traceability.

  • Lifetime Oy (2026). _DWS Security Standard_, version 1.5. `DWS_SECURITY_STANDARD.md`. Internal framework governing AI safety, middleware security controls, CORS policies, SRI integrity, Article 12 logging, and API boundary validation using Zod schemas. Establishes the security architecture for all 15 backend API routes and the MCP agent server.
  • Lifetime Oy (2026). _ESRS Data Point Registry_, version 1.0. `src/compliance/esrs-registry.ts`. Machine-readable mapping of all 82 European Sustainability Reporting Standards (ESRS) disclosure requirements across E1-E5 (Environment), S1-S4 (Social), and G1 (Governance). Each disclosure is classified as mandatory or voluntary under the CSRD Directive (EU) 2022/2464.
  • Lifetime Oy (2026). _Compliance Scoring Engine_, version 1.0. `src/compliance/scoring-engine.ts`. Deterministic compliance scoring algorithm computing CSRD readiness, ETS exposure, and CBAM risk scores. Formula: overall = CSRD(0.50) + ETS(0.25) + CBAM(0.25), where CSRD = (mandatory data points provided / total mandatory) x 80 + bonus modifiers.
  • Lifetime Oy (2026). _Site Quality Standards_, version 2.1. `SITE_QUALITY_STANDARDS.md`. Code quality, component architecture, and UI resilience patterns for the DWS IQ dashboard. Defines ErrorBoundary, Skeleton loading, and EmptyState component standards for enterprise-grade user experience.
  • Lifetime Oy (2026). _AGENTS.md_, version 2.2. `AGENTS.md`. Agent inventory and operational guidance following the agents.md open specification. Documents 15 AI agents, the compliance scoring engine architecture, middleware stack ordering, and the "Build for Agents" product rule requiring CLI + MCP + Skills interfaces.
  • Lifetime Oy (2026). _DWS IQ Platform Admin Manual_, version 2.7. `Admin/ADMIN_MANUAL.md`. Operational procedures for health check monitoring, compliance scoring engine operations, and dashboard resilience troubleshooting. Includes the Claude Code Project Engineering Structure and Multi-Agent Orchestration sections.
  • Lifetime Oy (2026). _Enterprise Security & Compliance_, version 1.8. `Admin/SECURITY_COMPLIANCE.md`. Security controls for CISOs and procurement teams. Documents agent runtime security, dashboard resilience controls, backend middleware security controls, and compliance engine data protection with RLS policies.
  • LUT University (2026). _Tekoälytyökalujen käytänteet_ [AI Tool Usage Policies]. Lappeenrannan-Lahden teknillinen yliopisto LUT. https://elut.lut.fi/fi/opintojen-suorittaminen/opintoja-ohjaavat-saadokset-ja-ohjeet/tekoalytyokalujen-kaytanteet — University-level policy framework for AI tool usage in academic work, requiring transparent disclosure of AI assistance and documenting the human-AI collaboration model.

    Appendix E: Automated PR Workflow (HIL Automation)

    The Firehorse operations model includes a fully automated pull request review and merge workflow that replaces manual human-in-the-loop code review. This is a key operational efficiency gain for solo developers and small teams managing enterprise-grade codebases.

    Architecture: 
    Claude Code → PR → Claude sanity check → Gemini HIL review → @copilot fixes → CI → auto-merge
                                                ↑                    |
                                                └────────────────────┘  (fix loop)
    Components:
    ComponentRoleTechnology
    Claude CodeCode generation, branch creation, PR openingAnthropic Claude Opus/Sonnet
    AI Code ReviewDual-model review replacing human reviewerGitHub Actions workflow
    Claude (reviewer)Quick sanity check: security, correctness, breaking changesClaude Sonnet 4 API
    Gemini (HIL gate)Deep review: edge cases, tests, architecture, OWASP securityGemini 2.0 Flash API
    Copilot coding agentImplements fix instructions from review commentsGitHub Copilot
    PR Auto-MergeMerges when all checks pass and AI approvesGitHub Actions + GraphQL
    Key design decision: Gemini defaults to REQUEST_CHANGES on every review. This ensures every PR goes through at least one improvement cycle, enforcing quality even when the original implementation appears correct. Only code with zero issues, adequate tests, and convention compliance receives APPROVE. Operational metrics (expected):
  • Developer involvement reduced to final skim/approve (~5 min per PR)
  • 70-90% of basic/refactor/test issues caught before human sees them
  • Typical fix loop: 1-3 iterations before approval
    • Deployment: Cloud Run service `dws6run6` (europe-north1, Finland). MCP Hub `POST /gemini/review` for on-demand code analysis.

    14.9 Climate IQ Pantheon — Greek/Roman God Multi-Agent System

    System Overview

    Tier 1 — Orchestrator Agents (6)

    AthenaGreek GoddessEU ComplianceMemorized every EU regulation since 2005
    HephaestusGreek GodField & SiteNever gets mud on his boots, but checks everything
    IrisGreek GoddessResearch & IntelReads 10,000 pages so you don't have to

    SOUL.md Pattern

    Each agent has a `SOUL.md` identity file injected as LLM system prompt via `BaseAgent.callLLM(systemPromptOverride)`. This resolves the "SOUL dead code" audit finding where persona constants were defined but never wired into LLM calls.

    Security & Compliance

  • Prompt injection fix: Replaced all `...input` spreads in HermesAgent with explicit typed field extraction
  • EU AI Act Article 12: All 13 agents write to `agent_executions` Supabase table (start + completion events)

    • Business Innovation: Names as Marketing

    The Pantheon naming is the product's marketing wedge — agent one-liners appear on onelifetime.world/team alongside human staff, making enterprise AI approachable without sacrificing credibility.

    Appendix F: Claude Code Engineering Structure — AI Workflow as First-Class Engineering

    The Firehorse operations model is built on a principle that emerges from both the research findings and operational practice: AI workflows must be engineered, not improvised. This appendix documents the concrete structural patterns that implement this principle in the DWS6 repository, validated against emerging Claude Code community best practices (2026). CLAUDE.md as Project Memory The `CLAUDE.md` file at the repository root is not documentation — it is the primary intelligence substrate for every AI agent session. It encodes standards, constraints, architecture decisions, email rules, security controls, and non-negotiables in a single file that every Claude Code session reads automatically. This eliminates behavioral drift and removes the need for repeated per-session context injection. Key metric: 2,000+ lines of institutional knowledge (brand standards, document standards, security controls, deployment rules, thesis rules, email rules) inherited by every session at zero marginal cost. Skills as Reusable Workflow Components (expanded March 2026) By March 2026, the skills inventory grew from 6 to 21 production skills across four categories:

  • 17 slash commands in `.claude/commands/`: gstack development workflow (6), EU compliance (4), agent control (3), operations (3), code quality (2)
  • 4 discipline skills in `skills//SKILL.md`: `verification-gate` (blocks "done" claims without evidence), `design-first` (blocks 3+ file changes without approved design), `dual-folder-sync` (blocks root changes without landing-pages sync), `last30days` (research agent)
  • 4 specialized subagents in `.claude/agents/`: `mcp-expert`, `code-reviewer`, `security-reviewer`, `architect`
  • Secret scanning hook: PreToolUse hook scanning 14 secret patterns (AWS, GitHub, Stripe, Anthropic, etc.) with audit logging
    • The discipline skills represent a novel contribution: blocking gates enforced via hook exit code 2 (BLOCK) that cannot be bypassed by agent behavior. This is structural enforcement, not behavioral suggestion — essential for regulated industries where "the agent usually follows the rules" is insufficient. Hooks for Automated Quality Enforcement Git hooks (`.githooks/`) enforce structural invariants:
  • Branch naming convention (`claude/-`) — prevents incorrect branch workflows
  • Anti-destructive-push guard — prevents accidental deletion of production branches
  • Post-merge sync — keeps Admin/, docs/, AgentFoundry/ synchronized to backup storage
    • The quality enforcement is structural, not behavioral — hooks cannot be bypassed by agent behavior. This is the key architectural distinction between a disciplined AI workflow system and an ad-hoc one. Subagents for Context Isolation and Parallel Execution Background agents enable parallel research and task execution without exhausting the main context window. The Explore agent specifically provides codebase search without polluting the primary context — critical for large repositories. This pattern directly extends the multi-agent orchestration documented in Sections 3 and 14. Conclusion The architectural lesson for the Firehorse Operations Model: the difference between AI experiments and AI products is not the model — it is the engineering discipline applied to how the model is integrated. CLAUDE.md, skills, hooks, and subagents are not features; they are the infrastructure that makes the Firehorse model reliable, consistent, and scalable.

    Section 14.10: Agent System Hardening — Production Reliability

    The 31-agent system accumulated 6 TypeScript compilation errors and 3 test failures across module boundaries. These were not design flaws but integration debt — the kind that emerges when independently correct modules evolve at different speeds.

    Fixes applied:
  • SandboxContext type alignment — `SandboxContext.stage` in `types.ts` was missing the `'opensandbox'` literal that `SandboxStage` in `sandbox/index.ts` defined. This blocked agent execution in Stage 1.5 (Acer Veriton Docker).
  • MCP route wiring — `createMCPRouter()` was defined in `src/mcp/agent-server.ts` but never imported in `app.ts`. All 31 agents were invisible to external MCP clients.
  • HIL route wiring — The Human-in-the-Loop endpoint (`/api/hil`) for morning briefing task completion was unreachable. Captain Pre-Briefing "Done" buttons returned 404.
  • Locale-deterministic formatting — Emissions Data Analyst used `toLocaleString()` without locale, producing space-separated numbers on EU servers. Fixed with explicit `'en-US'` locale for consistent output.
  • Resend batch type safety — Email service `.map()` call failed on `CreateBatchSuccessResponse` union type. Added runtime array detection.
  • Dependency hygiene — `luxon` was imported but missing from `package.json`.
    • Result: 0 TypeScript errors (was 6), 47/47 tests passing (was 44/47). Research insight: In multi-agent architectures, integration debt accumulates at module boundaries, not within modules. Systematic hardening sessions — touching multiple subsystems in a single commit — are more effective than per-module fixes because they restore cross-cutting invariants that individual modules cannot enforce alone.

    Section 14.11: PostgreSQL Columnar HTAP Architecture — DWS Data Standard

    The Firehorse operations model generates growing volumes of campaign events, agent execution logs, and audit trail data. Running analytics against transactional heap tables creates performance bottlenecks as data scales.

    Solution: Two-Tier HTAP — Aiven-first mandate (updated 13.03.2026)

    The original v2.2 plan assumed pg_duckdb availability on Aiven. Three market shifts invalidated that assumption:

  • Hydra joined Supabase (Feb 2026) — renamed "Supabase Warehouse"; no longer independent, not on Aiven.
  • pg_mooncake acquired by Databricks (Oct 2025) — roadmap pivoted to lakehouse; Aiven listing unlikely.
  • pg_duckdb: BLOCKED on Aiven whitelist — extension not in Aiven's approved list; no ETA for inclusion.
    • Extension evaluation (revised):
    ExtensionEngineAiven StatusVerdict
    TimescaleDB 2.25.xPostgreSQL (Hypercore)ApprovedONLY managed option — hypertables + columnar compression
    pg_duckdbDuckDBBLOCKED (not whitelisted)Edge-only — approved for GN100 self-managed nodes
    HydraCitus columnarDead — joined Supabase (Feb 2026)Removed from evaluation
    pg_mooncakeDuckDBDead — acquired by Databricks (Oct 2025)Removed from evaluation
    Recommended stack — two-tier HTAP:
    TierHostExtensionsRole
    ProductionAiven PostgreSQL (EU)TimescaleDB 2.25.x HypercoreOLTP + time-series compression, audit logs, agent metrics
    Edge analyticsAcer Veriton GN100 (ARM64)TimescaleDB + pg_duckdb + pgvectorLocal OLAP, fleet telemetry, AI inference

    Edge PostgreSQL deployed 2026-03-13 on Acer Veriton GN100 (ARM64), self-managed.

    DWS Data Standard (`DWS_DATA_STANDARD.md`) governing:
  • Storage engine decisions (row vs columnar vs hybrid)
  • Extension governance with EU sovereignty assessment and Aiven whitelist compliance
  • Retention policies mapped to 11 EU regulations (GDPR Art 17, EU AI Act Art 12: 7yr, NIS2: 5yr, Finnish Accounting Act: 10yr)
  • Partitioning and compression standards
    • Revised rollout: Phase 0 (table partitioning) → Phase 1 (TimescaleDB Hypercore on Aiven) → Phase 2 (edge pg_duckdb on GN100) → Phase 3 (Aiven↔GN100 replication). Research contribution: Demonstrates that managed-provider extension whitelists are a first-class architectural constraint — not a deployment detail. When the preferred extension (pg_duckdb) is blocked, the architecture must split into tiers rather than compromise on provider choice. The two-tier pattern (managed cloud + self-managed edge) preserves both EU data residency and analytical capability.

    14.12 Harness Engineering and Forensic Telemetry

    Source: OpenAI, "Harness engineering: leveraging Codex in an agent-first world", 13.02.2026; analysis in `docs/OPENAI_HARNESS_ENGINEERING_ANALYSIS.md`. Context: OpenAI's harness engineering framework describes how the environment surrounding AI agents (scaffolding, constraints, feedback loops) determines agent effectiveness more than the model itself. This section connects harness engineering to Firehorse's forensic telemetry and the broader PHD research on court-defensible AI operations.

    14.12.1 The Harness-Forensics Connection

    OpenAI's harness model focuses on developer productivity — building products faster with AI agents. DWS IQ 6 extends the harness concept to forensic operations, where every agent action must be traceable, auditable, and court-defensible.

    Harness LayerOpenAI PurposeFirehorse Forensic Purpose
    AGENTS.mdMap for developer agentsAudit-ready system description for regulators
    Golden PrinciplesCode quality enforcementCompliance standard adherence across 20 industries
    Knowledge in repoDeveloper documentationVERSION AND AGENT HISTORY — immutable modification log
    Agent-agent reviewCode quality iterationMulti-model review creating forensic evidence trail
    Feedback loopsDeveloper productivityCourt-defensible decision provenance
    Key insight: The DWS Document Standard's VERSION AND AGENT HISTORY block transforms every document into a forensic artifact. Each modification records: version number, tool used, session ID, date, and specific changes. This is a harness mechanism that OpenAI does not describe — because they optimize for speed, not legal defensibility.

    14.12.2 KYA Standard as Hardware-Attested Harness

    OpenAI's harness operates entirely at the software level — constraints, linters, code review loops. DWS's KYA (Know Your Agent/Architecture) Standard extends the harness into hardware:

    Harness TypeOpenAIDWS KYA
    Agent identitySoftware-level session IDsHardware-attested agent identity (TPM/Secure Boot)
    Execution isolationContainer sandboxingHardware-isolated execution environments
    Audit immutabilityGit historyHardware-attested audit logs (tamper-evident)
    Trust boundaryProcess-levelHardware security module boundary

    The KYA Standard's Shield (security) and Sword (optimization) paradigm demonstrates that hardware-level harness provides guarantees that software harness cannot — particularly for EU-regulated industries where agent decisions carry legal liability.

    14.12.3 Empirical Validation: Harness Over Model

    The harness engineering literature provides empirical evidence supporting the Firehorse Operations Model:

  • 52.8% → 66.5% improvement from harness changes alone (LangChain case, same gpt-5.2-codex model)
  • 46.9% token savings from progressive disclosure (Cursor)
  • 15-47% better rule compliance from L6 Tool Result Injection vs system prompt alone (DWS internal measurement)

    • These findings validate the Firehorse model's investment in operational infrastructure (forensic telemetry, automated PR review, session handover protocol) over model selection. The model is interchangeable — the harness is the competitive advantage.

    14.12.4 EU Regulatory Divergence

    For Firehorse operations serving EU-regulated industries, the harness must satisfy constraints that OpenAI's internal tooling does not face:

    EU RequirementHarness ImplicationFirehorse Implementation
    EU AI Act Art 12 (7-year logging)Every agent action logged immutablyVERSION AND AGENT HISTORY + Supabase audit tables
    EU AI Act Art 14 (human oversight)Human-in-the-loop mandatory for high-riskKarpathy principles: "human is the architect"
    GDPR Art 17 (right to erasure)Data deletion while preserving audit trailPseudonymized audit logs with separate PII store
    NIS2 Art 23 (incident reporting)24-hour breach notification capabilityFirehorse automated incident telemetry
    Research contribution: This section demonstrates that harness engineering is not one-size-fits-all. The EU regulatory context requires a fundamentally different harness design — one that prioritizes traceability, human oversight, and legal defensibility over speed. OpenAI's 3.5 PR/engineer/day metric is irrelevant when the primary metric is compliance assurance and audit readiness.

    14.13 LWM Sequencing Strategy — From Regulatory Intelligence to Physical AI

    Source: LeWorldModel (arXiv:2603.19312, Maes et al., March 2026); LWM sequencing analysis in `docs/field-notes/2026-03-25-lwm-phase2-technical-gap-analysis.md`. Context: The Lifetime World Model serves dual roles: regulatory intelligence engine (Phase 1, current) and physical planning system (Phase 2, future). This section documents the sequencing strategy and the technical gap between phases, framing the PhD research agenda.

    14.13.1 Three-Phase Sequencing

    Phase 1 (Current): LWM as regulatory intelligence engine — 15,000+ EU documents, 18 AI agents, carbon balance sheet with cross-regulation reconciliation. Revenue-generating. Validated by 6 customers (KELA, Taksi Helsinki, Uber, Bolt, Limo Helsinki, Sixt). Phase 2 (Post-SAFE): LWM adds physical planning via latent space models (LeWorldModel-style JEPA architecture). Helsinki healthcare fleet pilot. The carbon ledger provides regulatory constraints for physical planning decisions. Phase 3 (Series A): Full dual-use product — compliance infrastructure + edge Physical AI across 5 industries. Key insight for PhD research: Compliance data feeds physical planning. Not the reverse. This inverts the typical world model narrative and represents a novel architectural contribution.

    14.13.2 LeWorldModel as External Validation

    LeWorldModel (Maes, Le Lidec, Scieur, LeCun, Balestriero, 2026) demonstrates stable JEPA training from raw pixels using only 2 loss terms (prediction + SIGReg). 15M parameters, single GPU, 48x faster planning than foundation model alternatives. The SIGReg regularizer (Cramer-Wold theorem, isotropic Gaussian prior) solves the representation collapse problem that plagued prior JEPA approaches.

    This validates the world model category but does not validate Lifetime's specific product. The honest framing: Lifetime builds domain-specific regulatory infrastructure on commodity AI.

    14.13.3 Phase 2 Technical Gap

    Three unsolved problems for PhD investigation:

  • Input modality gap: Text-based regulatory graph → latent planner expecting visual/sensor input. Proposed bridge: OpenUSD digital twin encoding both physical and regulatory metadata.
  • Scale gap: LeWorldModel tested in controlled simulation (PushT, Reacher). Industrial environments are uncontrolled, long-horizon, sparse-reward. Scalability is research.
  • Regulatory-physical integration: Physical planner proposes → regulatory engine validates → KYA governance enforces. KYA v1.4 (Firecracker MicroVM kill-switches) is the only Phase 2 component already built.
    • Research contribution: The compliance-first world model thesis — that regulatory infrastructure must precede physical planning capability — provides a concrete research programme connecting LeWorldModel's theoretical advances to industrial deployment in regulated EU environments.

    14.14 Competitive Analysis: Domain-Specific vs Generic AI Harness

    A March 2026 benchmarking study compared the DWS6 harness architecture against everything-claude-code (ECC) — a 120,000-star open-source framework providing 136 skills, 30 subagents, and 60 slash commands.

    The fundamental trade-off: ECC optimizes for breadth (136 skills × 8 languages × 5 IDEs); DWS6 optimizes for depth (21 skills × deep EU regulatory knowledge × structural enforcement).

    Six competitive differentiators identified:

  • Domain-specific skills encoding EU regulation (ETS, CBAM, CSRD, EN 15978, EN 13001) — not replicable by generic skill count
  • Six-layer information architecture (L0-L6) controlling context injection at every layer — ECC operates at L2-L3 only
  • Hard gates (verification-gate, design-first, dual-folder-sync) using exit code 2 (BLOCK) — ECC skills are advisory-only
  • Trust boundary zones (GREEN/YELLOW/RED) with risk-proportionate action classification — ECC has binary secret scanning only
  • gstack workflow with enforced cognitive role separation — ECC skills are independent and unorchestrated
  • Agent-first product rule (5-gate shipping checklist: CLI, JSON output, MCP tool, Claude skill, markdown docs) — skills are product interfaces, not developer convenience
    • Research significance: At the token budget level, breadth is counterproductive for regulated domains. Loading 136 generic skills consumes context window capacity that domain-specific agents need for regulatory reasoning. The DWS6 constraint of <150 words per discipline skill is a token budget optimization enabling deeper domain reasoning. This validates the harness engineering thesis (Section 14.12): a 21-skill domain-specific harness outperforms a 136-skill generic harness when regulatory precision matters.

    14.15 Master Thesis Dual-Version Architecture

    The master thesis (V3Thesis) grew to 82 pages and 26,000+ words during the AI-assisted research process — nearly double the 40-50 page Metropolia submission target. Rather than permanently deleting research content, a dual-version architecture was adopted:

    v3.30.50 (Frozen, 50 pages): Restructured for Metropolia submission. Sections 10.6-10.18 consolidated into a summary section (10.6 "Extended Research Contributions") with cross-references to this PHD Firehorse document. Section 5 expanded from 0.7 to ~4 pages. Appendices trimmed from 8 to 4. This file receives only HIL (Human-in-the-Loop) edits — no automated AI updates. v3.32+ (Living, 82 pages): Continues accumulating research findings as before. Every new version entry is a timestamped record of research progression. PHD Firehorse as canonical home: Sections 14.1-14.14 of this document now serve as the authoritative detailed documentation for content summarized in the master thesis v3.30.50. The master thesis explicitly cross-references "PHD Firehorse Sections 14.X" for each summarized topic. Research contribution: This dual-version pattern demonstrates a practical methodology for AI-assisted academic writing where research velocity systematically exceeds institutional format constraints. The pattern preserves intellectual property while meeting submission requirements — a recurring challenge as AI accelerates research documentation. Key Contribution #18: Dual-version thesis architecture as methodological contribution to AI-assisted academic writing.

    14.16 April 2026 Evolution: Report Engine in Production, Product Line V2, Snap Benchmark, and Founder→Team Transition

    _Added: 20.04.2026 | Source: PRs #1064, #1109, #1110; field notes 2026-04-06 through 2026-04-19; Firehorse Snap benchmark (Firecracker MicroVM); SOLO_FOUNDER_STATEMENT v1.2._

    The Firehorse Report module graduated from specification to scheduled production. PR #1064 merged the report scheduler (`src/firehorse/report-scheduler.ts`), module-scoped tests (`src/__tests__/firehorse-report-module.test.ts`), and a manual trigger route. This is the first Firehorse module to ship a cron-driven customer deliverable, closing the loop between the Operations Model (§14.1–14.4) and customer-facing value:

    CapabilityImplementation

    Manual triggerREST route for ad-hoc report generation
    Test coverageModule-scoped tests (no cross-module leakage)

    Operations-model significance: The scheduler is the first Firehorse component where the 3-tier billing contract (§2.0 v2.0, Version 2.0) produces recurring compliance artefacts without human intervention. The hypothesis that an AI-assisted one-person team can operate a multi-tenant compliance product is now testable against real scheduled dispatch.

    14.16.2 Firehorse Product Line V2 — Unified DWS IQ Product Family

    In April 2026 the four Firehorse modules (Omnichannel, Signed Report, Automation, Content Management) were consolidated into a single V2 product line under the DWS IQ product family. Each module received a V2 naming, dedicated imagery, and a store presence. Merged commits include `Firehorse Product Line V2`, `Firehorse Automation V2`, `Firehorse Starter V2 bundle`, `Firehorse Content Management V2`, `Firehorse Signed Report V2`, `Firehorse ReportV2`, and `firehorse-omnichannel-v2.png`. The Firehorse Starter V2 bundle exists as an entry-level SKU.

    Operations-model significance: V2 crystallises the Solution Factory output into a catalogue-first product family that can be purchased, bundled, and versioned independently — a pre-condition for the Ops Manager ICP (see §14.16.5).

    14.16.3 Firehorse Snap Benchmark — <125 ms Measured Kill Latency

    Earlier versions of this thesis cited 122 ms as the Firehorse Snap kill latency specification from Arctic 15 planning documents. In April 2026, the measured kill latency on Firecracker MicroVM infrastructure was captured and published on the KYA Standard page (`dws10.com/kya-standard`). The public claim has been updated from the 122 ms spec to <125 ms measured (Firecracker MicroVM benchmark). The KYA page now displays both measured and Firecracker spec figures so readers can distinguish specification from measurement.

    Operations-model significance: Court-defensible harness claims (§14.12 Harness Engineering and Forensic Telemetry) require measurement, not specification. Updating the public figure from spec to measured is the first time a Firehorse harness guarantee is backed by a reproducible benchmark. This is a precondition for EU AI Act Article 12 log admissibility in enforcement proceedings.

    14.17 DWS Skilled Taxonomy: Brand / Spec / UI Across Agents, Workflows, and Jurisdictions

    _Added: 22.04.2026 | Source: CLAUDE.md "DWS Skilled Taxonomy Rule"; Anthropic 2026-04-16 Skills paradigm essay (§14.16.4); deprecation of "GodAgent" branding. PHD_FIREHORSE version track unified with Metropolia V3Thesis at v3.37; from v3.37 forward all 4 thesis files bump in lockstep (3.37 → 3.38 → … → 3.99 → 4.00)._

    14.17.1 From "GodAgent" to a Three-Tier Naming System

    The Firehorse Operations Model earlier used "GodAgent" as a single label for industry-scoped specialist agents.
